    Authors: Jiahao Xu, Peiyuan Wang, Hanzhuo Zhang, Zihao Yu, Tianyu Fu, Hao Chen, Xuanhao Xiang, Zixuan Li, Jianbo Yu, Chenchen Fu, Wanyuan Wang

To enable efficient long-horizon manipulation, we propose GTP-FA (Grasp-Then-Plan with Failure Attribution), a task-oriented two-stage 'grasp-then-plan' framework that generates grasp candidates and performs downstream motion planning conditioned on the selected grasp. Given a failed manipulation trajectory, we learn a failure attribution model that generalizes to unseen grasps and produces a stable distribution over failure modes for diagnosis-guided optimization. Based on these attribution results, we then optimize both modules in a diagnosis-driven manner: on the grasping side, we inject task-level priors and risk penalties into grasp candidate scoring and optimization to suppress unstable or task-incompatible grasps; on the planning side, we target high-risk initial states through data collection and fine-tuning to address genuine planning bottlenecks. We evaluate the proposed framework in both simulation and real-robot experiments, and show that GTP-FA improves the corresponding base learners across RL, IL, diffusion-policy, and VLA-based settings, achieving substantially higher overall task success rates. △ Less

With the rising computational cost of training large-scale deep models, dataset distillation has emerged as a promising approach for constructing compact synthetic datasets that retain knowledge from large original datasets. Although optimization-based methods can achieve strong performance, they often incur considerable computational and memory costs and may produce synthetic images that generalize poorly across architectures. Recent non-optimization-based methods alleviate these limitations by constructing distilled images from real-image patches. However, their inflexible patch-construction strategies may either retain redundant background content or discard important foreground structures. To address this issue, we propose a foreground-aware dataset distillation framework with content-adaptive dual-path patch selection. We first use Grounded SAM2 to estimate the foreground occupancy of each image and derive category-specific routing thresholds from the corresponding class-wise distributions. Each image is routed to one of two complementary paths: the candidate-crop selection path for images with relatively sparse foregrounds, or the direct transformation path for images with relatively dominant foregrounds. This dual-path mechanism balances foreground concentration and preservation, reducing redundant background content while retaining the structural integrity of foreground objects at the dataset level. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, ImageNette, and ImageWoof with two network architectures and three distillation budgets demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms the baseline method RDED in nearly all evaluated settings. △ Less

    Authors: Yi Fu, Natalia L. Komarova

    Abstract: …limit where migration occurs on a much slower timescale than within-deme dynamics, the full stochastic process can be reduced to a lower-dimensional Markov chain whose states correspond to configurations of fully mutant and fully wild-type demes. The reduction theorem establishes that fixation probabilities and absorption times of the original process are as… ▽ More Population fragmentation is a common feature of many biological systems. Understanding mutant fixation in such systems is challenging because the underlying stochastic dynamics are high-dimensional. In this work, we develop a general mathematical framework for analyzing stochastic evolution in fragmented populations connected by rare migration. The framework is sufficiently general to accommodate heterogeneous deme sizes, deme-dependent birth and death processes, and migration on arbitrary strongly connected directed networks with asymmetric migration rates. We show that, in the limit where migration occurs on a much slower timescale than within-deme dynamics, the full stochastic process can be reduced to a lower-dimensional Markov chain whose states correspond to configurations of fully mutant and fully wild-type demes. The reduction theorem establishes that fixation probabilities and absorption times of the original process are asymptotically determined by the corresponding quantities of a reduced chain. As an application, we derive explicit formulas for mutant fixation probabilities and fixation times in fragmented populations initiated by the introduction of a single mutant. The results provide a general and tractable approach for studying evolutionary dynamics in complex fragmented populations. △ Less

    quant-ph cs.LG stat.ML

    Advantage of Sample Complexity in Quantum PAC Learning Requires Inverse Access to State-Preparation Unitaries

    Authors: Natsuto Isogai, Satoshi Yoshida, Mio Murao

    Abstract: …setting. Beyond PAC learning, our reduction provides a general tool for transferring lower bounds for state copies to query lower bounds for forward-only access to the corresponding preparation unitaries. ▽ More Whether quantum computation can reduce the amount of data required for conventional machine learning is a central problem in quantum machine learning. In probably approximately correct (PAC) learning, a learner receives training examples sampled from an unknown probability distribution. Quantum PAC learning extends this framework by allowing access to quantum examples, which are quantum states that coherently encode this distribution, or to unitaries that prepare quantum examples, with or without inverse access. When only copies of quantum examples are available, the optimal worst-case sample complexity asymptotically matches that of classical learning. In contrast, access to both a preparation unitary and its inverse can improve the accuracy dependence of query complexity. However, it has remained open whether access to the preparation unitary alone suffices for this improvement. In this work, we resolve this question by determining the optimal query complexity with forward-only access, taking the worst case over compatible preparation unitaries and their dimensions. In both realizable and agnostic PAC learning, this complexity matches, up to constant factors, the optimal sample complexity achievable with classical examples or copies of quantum examples. To prove these results, we construct, for any algorithm with forward-only access, a simulator that approximates the algorithm's Haar-averaged output state using as many copies of the prepared state as the algorithm makes queries. These results show that inverse access is essential for the known improvement in accuracy dependence in this worst-case setting. Beyond PAC learning, our reduction provides a general tool for transferring lower bounds for state copies to query lower bounds for forward-only access to the corresponding preparation unitaries. △ Less

    Stationary Action Principles for Neuronal Growth

    Authors: Nathan Brodeur, Cristian Staii

    Abstract: …deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction gene… ▽ More Neuronal growth is guided by biochemical, mechanical, and geometrical cues while being influenced by stochastic fluctuations in cytoskeletal dynamics, adhesion, and growth cone sensing. Here, we develop two complementary variational descriptions of mechanically guided axonal growth. First, for neurons growing on periodic parallel micropatterns, we use Langevin and Fokker--Planck models to derive a stochastic path action incorporating deterministic substrate guidance and random fluctuations. The probability of a growth trajectory decreases exponentially with this action, and the most probable trajectories correspond to minimum action paths for fixed boundary conditions. Second, for axons growing on substrates with spatially varying stiffness, we show that stiffness dependent traction generates mechanical guidance and axonal turning at stiffness interfaces. In the quasi-static limit, this response leads to a Fermat-like spatial action with an effective axonal refractive index determined by the traction and substrate stiffness relationship. The resulting framework predicts axonal refraction across stiffness boundaries, including an approximate Snell-like relation and a critical angle condition. Although the stochastic and Fermat-like actions have distinct physical meanings and ranges of validity, both describe how substrate mechanics constrain axonal trajectories. These results connect measurable quantities, including drift, diffusion, stiffness, traction, and growth direction, to global properties of axonal paths and provide a framework for designing biomimetic substrates for controlled neuronal growth and regeneration. △ Less

Sobolev IPM is intrinsically coupled with $L^p$ geometric structure within its definition, limiting its ability to incorporate other prior geometry beyond the $L^p$ paradigm. By further exploiting the underlying graph structure, we show that the regularized GSI reduces to a simple univariate optimization problem, achieving notably computational efficiency, enabling its usage in practical applications. We empirically illustrate that the regularized GSI is several-order faster than the popular OW in computation, and its performances compare favorably to other transport baselines for comparing graph-based measures in document classification and topological data analysis. △ Less

Finally, we construct some Hecke eigenfunctions using the relation to Higgs bundles over the corresponding reduced curve. △ Less

    Quark Saturation in Magnetized Pure Neutron Matter

    Authors: Toru Kojo

    Abstract: …transverse direction, and strongly suppresses the transverse kinetic energy. From the baryon wave functions, we construct the one-body reduced density matrix and derive the corresponding single-quark distributions in longitudinal momentum and Landau-level space. We then use these distributions to evaluate quark occupation probabilities in an ideal baryon gas… ▽ More We study how magnetic fields affect the onset of quark saturation, which limits the domain of applicability of a purely baryonic description. We consider matter composed of electrically neutral $ddu$ baryons, including neutron- and $Δ^0$-like states. Using a simple constituent-quark model, we compute the baryon wave functions and spectra in magnetic fields. The magnetic field polarizes the spin-flavor structure, compresses the internal wave function in the transverse direction, and strongly suppresses the transverse kinetic energy. From the baryon wave functions, we construct the one-body reduced density matrix and derive the corresponding single-quark distributions in longitudinal momentum and Landau-level space. We then use these distributions to evaluate quark occupation probabilities in an ideal baryon gas and determine the onset of quark saturation. At finite magnetic field in the $+z$-direction, the $d_\downarrow$ quark in the lowest Landau level reaches saturation first. The resulting saturation density depends nonmonotonically on the magnetic-field strength: it decreases at weak fields and increases at stronger fields. This behavior reflects the competition between spin-flavor polarization, which promotes saturation, and transverse spatial squeezing, which delays saturation by broadening of the quark transverse momentum distribution. △ Less

With in situ observations of the magnetic field on a DRO, the southward ($B_z < 0$) magnetic field of interplanetary coronal mass ejections (ICMEs), which is not routinely accessible remotely, could be measured several hours in advance. This $B_z < 0$ field is a decisive factor for forecasting geomagnetic storm intensity. We present an analysis of DROs at different sub-L1 distances for their efficacy for a space weather forecasting mission. An open-source numerical framework to generate DRO trajectories is presented. Our pre-calculated orbits in Heliocentric Earth Ecliptic coordinates can be introduced into numerical or empirical simulations of the ambient solar wind and ICMEs. We then analyze the corresponding forecast lead times, the resulting observational gaps depending on the number of spacecraft and distance of a DRO, and the "cone of acceptance" of the maximum longitudinal separation of a spacecraft to the Sun-Earth line. We define different domains regarding radial evolution and longitudinal CME structure, and show the historical coverage of sub-L1 ICME observations with our ICMECAT catalog. An optimal mission configuration can be selected based on the characteristics of DROs presented here, depending on the requirements of such a mission deemed acceptable with respect to results on CME evolution and coherence. △ Less

This is due to the tunability of the dielectric constant and dissipation factor in the underlying ferroelectric and paraelectric states. One important feature of BST is that the competition between the ferroelectric BaTiO3 and the paraelectric SrTiO3 parent phases can be tuned by the A-site alloy concentration, opening a platform for exploring dielectric phase competition. However, the understanding of the interplay between and establishing the control of the dielectric phase from thin film deposition parameters has not been sufficiently reported in the literature. We mapped the dielectric phase of the film to the oxygen growth pressure, film composition, and film crystal structure. A ferroelectric, relaxor-like, or paraelectric phase can be obtained with relatively moderate change in oxygen growth pressure. Scanning transmission electron microscopy (STEM) analysis further indicates that this pressure-dependent phase evolution is accompanied by a structural transition of the BST lattice from tetragonal toward a nearly cubic phase, with the latter corresponding to the relaxor-like state. The control of the dielectric phase opens a pathway to study phase evolution and enable future computing technologies. △ Less

A ray-conditioned kernel extends each primitive with a viewing-direction subspace that… ▽ More Monocular outdoor navigation requires robust camera motion estimation and a dense, navigable map, where 3D Gaussian Splatting SLAM serves this task through an explicit differentiable parameterization and closed-form projection. However, typically, the geometry is recovered by associating 2D pixels with 3D points, so a modular SLAM system absorbs tracking error into the map, leading to cascading degradation. To address these gaps, we present SG^2SLAM, which couples streaming spatial geometry with a ray-conditioned 3DGS kernel. Streaming spatial geometry replaces the per- frame cross-dimensional correspondence with a chunk-level metric prior that supervises tracking and mapping from one source. A ray-conditioned kernel extends each primitive with a viewing-direction subspace that senses the monocular camera viewpoints. The joint optimization over spatial geometry and poses ensures a globally consistent map and trajectory outcome. Experiments on the KITTI and Waymo Open datasets show that our SG^2SLAM surpasses the per-frame association baselines in the monocular outdoor navigation task. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance. △ Less

    Chebyshev-expanded covariant density functional from relativistic Brueckner theory

    Authors: Armen Sedrakian

    Abstract: We construct a density-dependent covariant energy density functional (CE-ME1) from full-Dirac-space relativistic Brueckner--Hartree--Fock (RBHF) self-energies and the corresponding equation of state. The space-like component $U_V$ is incorporated at the Fermi surface through reduced scalar and time-like self-energies, which are mapped onto local Hartree $σ$,… ▽ More We construct a density-dependent covariant energy density functional (CE-ME1) from full-Dirac-space relativistic Brueckner--Hartree--Fock (RBHF) self-energies and the corresponding equation of state. The space-like component $U_V$ is incorporated at the Fermi surface through reduced scalar and time-like self-energies, which are mapped onto local Hartree $σ$, $ω$, $δ$, and $ρ$ channels. The density-dependent couplings are represented by cubic Chebyshev polynomials over the density range $0.08\leqρ\leq0.48~{\rm fm}^{-3}$. A constrained thermodynamic calibration uses 199 RBHF energy-per-particle values from published microscopic computations at six isospin asymmetries, while maintaining positive, monotonic couplings. The Bonn-A RBHF saturation point, incompressibility $K_\infty=258$ MeV, symmetry energy $J=33.1$ MeV, and slope $L=65.2$ MeV are reproduced. The predicted curvature $K_{\rm sym}=33$ MeV differs from the published RBHF value of $-67$ MeV, illustrating the derivative sensitivity of the local reduction. The combined energy RMS is $0.613$ MeV, measuring model discrepancy rather than statistical uncertainty. Uniform matter remains mechanically stable and causal above saturation within the calibrated range. In cold charge-neutral $npeμ$ matter, CE-ME1 reasonably reproduces the interpolated RBHF pressure and proton fraction over most of the interval, although the pressure discrepancy increases near its upper edge. The DD-ME2 benchmark is stiffer and predicts a lower proton fraction at high density. △ Less

    Dr. Post-Training: A Data Regularization Perspective on LLM Post-Training

    Authors: Pingbang Hu, Xueshen Liu, Z. Morley Mao, Jiaqi W. Ma

    Abstract: …onto that feasible set. Standard training and existing data selection methods arise as special cases with different choices of the data-induced regularizer, and these methods correspond to different points on a bias-variance spectrum with different regularization strengths. Building on this view, we propose a family of methods offering a richer design space… ▽ More Data selection methods address a critical challenge in LLM post-training: effectively leveraging scarce, high-fidelity target data alongside abundant but imperfectly aligned general training data. In this work, we move beyond the data-selection framing and introduce Dr. Post-Training (Data-Regularized Post-Training), a novel framework that reconceptualizes general training data as a data-induced regularizer that prevents overfitting to the scarce target objective, rather than serving as a pool for selection. Specifically, our framework proposes that, at each training step, we construct a feasible set of model update directions using the general training data, and project the model update direction specified by the scarce target data onto that feasible set. Standard training and existing data selection methods arise as special cases with different choices of the data-induced regularizer, and these methods correspond to different points on a bias-variance spectrum with different regularization strengths. Building on this view, we propose a family of methods offering a richer design space and more flexible bias-variance tradeoffs. For practical LLM-scale use, we introduce careful system optimizations that realize these methods with minimal overhead. Extensive experiments across SFT, RLHF, and RLVR show that our methods outperform standard training and state-of-the-art data selection baselines, with the largest gains where the general and target data are most misaligned, and system benchmarks confirm their efficiency. △ Less

    Authors: Facundo Mémoli, Qingsong Wang

    Abstract: …value from below in the next dimension as the number of layers increases. For a compact metric space and $0<r<s$, we prove that the canonical inclusion from scale $r$ to scale $s$ is a homotopy equivalence whenever $[r,s)$ contains no diameter of a finite labelled configuration at which the diameter function has zero weak slope. On Riemannian manifolds, weak-slope stationarity implies Clarke criticality. At positive diameter, when the distances realizing the diameter are smooth, both are equivalent to first-order stationarity: the absence of a direction decreasing all these distances to first order. For closed connected smooth manifolds, the Clarke critical diameter spectrum has Hausdorff dimension zero, even at the cut locus. In the real-analytic case, it is finite at each fixed number of labels and countable over all label numbers. On the unit round sphere $S^m$, $m\geq1$, positive nonantipodal weak-slope stationary configurations are characterized by nonzero nonnegative equilibrium stresses. The least positive critical diameter is $\arccos(-1/(m+1))$, and the first accumulation point is $\arccos(-1/m)$. We lift every such stress through a spherical stack construction adapted from Lovász, producing stationary diameters that approach the original value from below in the next dimension as the number of layers increases. We also give example in general metric space showing that weak-slope stationary diameters need not correspond to changes in the homotopy type of Vietoris-Rips complexes. △ Less

    q-bio.NC

    Temporal smoothness constraints on efficient neurobiological codes imply temporal specialization

    Authors: John J. Vastola, Samuel J. Gershman

    Abstract: …slowly varying temporal features, we find surprising structure of mathematical interest: optimal codes have subsystems which represent orthogonal temporal features, and which correspond to the eigenfunctions of a Sturm-Liouville problem. ▽ More Neurobiological circuits may be energy efficient in part because there is a division of labor: different subsystems compute or represent different things. In light of this, it is interesting that a major difference between subsystems is the time scale on which they typically vary. Here, we argue that temporal smoothness constraints---the idea that it may be energetically costly for systems to vary much more quickly than their typical time scale---imply a certain division of labor with respect to temporal stimulus features. In particular, slow subsystems ought to represent slowly varying stimulus features, and fast subsystems ought to represent quickly varying features. We formulate a novel efficient coding model that we use to investigate this claim, and exactly solve for the optimal division of labor. In addition to finding that slower subsystems ought to represent more slowly varying temporal features, we find surprising structure of mathematical interest: optimal codes have subsystems which represent orthogonal temporal features, and which correspond to the eigenfunctions of a Sturm-Liouville problem. △ Less
